Compatibility complexes for the conformal-to-Einstein operator Thanks: Funding : IK’s was partially supported by GAČR project GA22-00091S and the Czech Academy of Sciences Research Plan RVO: 67985840. JŠ was supported by GAČR project GA22-00091S
Igor Khavkine, Josef Šilhan

Abstract
. The conformal-to-Einstein operator is a conformally invariant linear overdetermined differential operator whose non-vanishing solutions correspond to Einstein metrics within a conformal class. We construct compatibility complexes for this operator under natural genericity assumptions on the Weyl curvature in dimension n ≥ 4 n\geq 4 , which implies at most one independent solution. An analogous result for the projective-to-Ricci-flat operator is obtained as well. The construction is based on a method, previously proposed by one of the authors, that leverages existing symmetries and geometric properties of the starting operator. In this case the compatibility complexes consist of, respectively, conformally and projectively invariant operators. We also make some comments on how Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand sequences can be interpreted as compatibility complexes in the locally flat case, which may be of general interest.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
53C18, 35N10, 53B10

1.
Introduction

The study of conformally related Einstein metrics is a classical problem
[
6
]

as well as an active area of recent research, see, e.g., the monograph
[
4
]

or discussion of curvature obstructions
[
17
]
.
Conformal geometry
(M,[g])
on a smooth manifold
M
is a class of conformally related metrics (of any signature)

[g]=\{e^{2\Upsilon}g\}
for
\Upsilon\in C^{\infty}(M)
positive everywhere and
n=\dim M\geq 3
.
Einstein metrics in
[g]
are locally controlled by the so-called
conformal-to-Einstein
operator

(1)

E_{0}\colon\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],\quad\sigma\mapsto\bigl(\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b)_{0}}+\operatorname{P}_{(ab)_{0}}\bigl)\sigma.

This operator is linear, conformally invariant, overdetermined, and solutions
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}[1]
without zeros are in 1-1 correspondence with Einstein metrics
\hat{g}
in
[g]
.
Specifically, if we identify
\sigma
with a function using the Riemannian volume form of
g
then

\hat{g}=\sigma^{-2}g
.
We use the notation
\mathcal{E}[w]
,
w\in\mathbb{R}
for the bundle of ordinary
(-\frac{w}{n})
-densities,
i.e.,
\Lambda^{n}T^{*}M=\mathcal{E}[-n]
,

\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
g
,
(-)_{0}
denotes the trace-free part and
\operatorname{P}_{ab}

denotes the Schouten tensor of
g
. We also use the abstract index notation, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{a}=T^{*}M
,
\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}=S^{2}T^{*}M
,
\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}=\Lambda^{2}T^{*}M
etc.
We put
\mathcal{E}_{a}[w]:=\mathcal{E}_{a}\otimes\mathcal{E}[w]
and similarly for more complicated tensor bundles.
More generally, all differential operators that we will consider below
will be linear, with smooth coefficients, and mapping between some
vector bundles over some fixed manifold
M
. We will denote the
composition of two differential operators
K
and
L
by
K\circ L
, or
simply by
KL
, if no confusion is possible.

After the obvious question of finding the solutions of a differential equation
such as
E_{0}(\sigma)=0
, another important question is to determine the range
of the operator
E_{0}
. For instance, analyzing the image of the exterior derivative operator
d
on forms leads in a sense to the de Rham complex and de Rham cohomology.
In another context, when analyzing the symmetries of a
geometric PDE, such as the Laplace or Dirac equations, intermediate
calculations run into the question of whether a certain tensor is in the image
of a given differential operator. Specific examples lead, e.g., the exterior derivative on functions
[
21
]
or the conformal Killing operator on
vector fields
[
2
]
.
In part, we are motivated to look at the operator
E_{0}
as a warm-up to studying similar more complicated operators, as approached not from the direction of maximal symmetry (or flatness), but from the direction of more generic geometries.

According to the theory of
overdetermined PDEs
[
23
,
7
]
, the image of a linear differential operator
like
E_{0}
acting on smooth functions can be locally characterized as the
kernel of another differential operator, say
E_{1}
, its so-called

compatibility operator
(cf. Definition
4.2
for the precise
notion that we will use below). When dealing with compatibility operators, it
is actually convenient to determine at the same time a whole sequence of them,

E_{k}
being the compatibility complex of
E_{k-1}
, the so-called

compatibility complex
. In this work, we will explicitly construct a
compatibility complex for the conformal-to-Einstein operator
E_{0}

(Section
5
) and a closely related operator from projective
geometry (Section
6
) under certain curvature assumptions.

An explicit construction of compatibility complexes is mostly known only for a geometrical setting
with symmetries. A famous example is the Calabi complex for the Killing operator on Riemannian metrics
of constant sectional curvature
[
8
,
13
,
18
]
.
From a modern point of view, this is an example of the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand (BGG)
complex in the flat projective geometry. More generally, we can consider BGG sequences in parabolic geometries

[
25
,
9
]
. In the locally flat case, any such sequence provides a compatibility complex of the first operator (cf. Section
4.3
).
These (overdetermined) operators are known as
first BGG operators
and (
1
) is a prominent example.
A BGG sequence will not provide a compatibility complex beyond the locally flat case, however, it will even fail to be a complex.
In fact, almost nothing is known about compatibility complexes for first BGG operators in curved parabolic
geometries. Compatibility complexes for Killing operators on certain (pseudo)Riemannian manifolds of physical and mathematical interest in General Relativity are known
[
19
,
1
]
; they were in fact constructed by the same methods that we will use in this work.
Furthermore, a partial result was obtained in
[
11
,
12
]

where the first two operators of a compatibility complex (i.e., the Killing operator
and its compatibility operator) were obtained on locally symmetric spaces.

Our main result is a compatibility complex for
E_{0}
in (
1
) in suitably generic cases as follows. Assuming
n\geq 4
,
the conformal geometry
(M,[g])
is locally flat if and only if the Weyl tensor

W_{abcd}
vanishes. As the ‘opposite’ case, we shall call
(M,[g])

generic
if the bundle map
\mathcal{E}^{d}\to\mathcal{E}_{abc}

induced by
W_{abcd}
is injective. Then the solution space of (
1
) is at most 1-dimensional,
cf. Section
3.2
for details.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume
(M,[g])
is generic and the solution space of (
1
) is 1-dimensional. Then
there is a compatibility complex for
E_{0}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}[1]\end{smallmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{E}_{a}[1]\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{E}_{[abc]}[1]\end{smallmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{[abcd]}[1].}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{[abcde]}[1]\hbox to0.0pt{~$\cdots$.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathrm{id}-D_{1}C_{1}\\
\tilde{d}\,C_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&-H^{\prime}_{1}\\
0&\tilde{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\tilde{d}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{d}}

Here the operators

\displaystyle C_{1}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],

\displaystyle D_{1}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{a}\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],

\displaystyle\tilde{d}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{[a_{0}\ldots a_{k}]}[1],

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{1}\colon

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1]

are defined in Section
3.3
. All these operators are
conformally invariant. The rest of the complex is just the twisted
exterior
\tilde{d}
acting on forms twisted with
\mathcal{E}[1]
.

In addition, we also obtain a compatibility complex when
E_{0}
has 0-dimensional solution space
in Section
5.1
.
This complex is much shorter – there are only three operators (including
E_{0}
).
The latter case can be also adapted to dimension 3. This in particular means
that the problem of a compatibility complex for
E_{0}
is fully solved in dimension 3 for
any signature and dimension 4 in the Riemannian signature, cf. discussion
in Remark
5.3
.

In Section
2
we quickly summarize our conventions and relevant facts from conformal geometry. Then, Section
3
presents all properties of the conformal-to-Einstein operator
E_{0}
that will be relevant for our main results. Section
4
briefly recalls the basic ideas about compatibility complexes and summarizes the construction method from
[
19
]
, which works for operators that are uniformly of finite type, of which our
E_{0}
and its projective analog are examples. Finally, in Section
5
we give the proof of our main result (Theorem
1.1
) for
generic
conformal structures, while in Section
6
, we obtain an analogous result for the projective analog of
E_{0}
(Theorem
6.1
).

2.
Conformal geometry

Let
(M,[g])
be a conformal manifold of the dimension
n\geq 3
and any
signature
(p,q)
where
n=p+q
. We shall use the notation mentioned in the beginning of Section
1
.
The conformal structure
gives rise to the
conformal metric

\bm{g}_{ab}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}[2]
with the inverse
\bm{g}^{ab}\in\mathcal{E}^{(ab)}[-2]
.
We have
\nabla_{a}\bm{g}_{bc}=0
for all metrics (and corresponding Levi-Civita connections
\nabla
) in
[g]
.
Further we have the conformal volume form
\bm{\epsilon}_{a_{1}\ldots a_{n}}\in\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{n}]}[n]
which is also parallel.

Denoting by
\nabla
and
\hat{\nabla}
the Levi-Civita connection corresponding to
g_{ab}
and

\hat{g}_{ab}=e^{2\Upsilon}g_{ab}
,
respectively, and putting
\Upsilon_{a}=\nabla_{a}\Upsilon
, we have

(2)

\begin{split}&\hat{\nabla}_{a}\sigma=\nabla_{a}\sigma+w\Upsilon_{a}\sigma,\\
&\hat{\nabla}_{a}\tau_{b}=\nabla_{a}\tau_{b}-\Upsilon_{a}\tau_{b}-\Upsilon_{b}\tau_{a}+\Upsilon^{r}\tau_{r}\bm{g}_{ab}\end{split}

where
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}[w]
and
\tau_{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{b}
. An analogous relation between
\hat{\nabla}_{a}
and
\nabla_{a}

on general tensor bundles can be computed using the Leibniz rule.
We denote by
R_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}
the Riemann curvature tensor of
\nabla
, by
W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}
its Weyl tensor, by

Ric_{ab}=(n-2)P_{ab}+g_{ab}J
the Ricci tensor, which also gives its relation to the Schouten tensor
P_{ab}
and its trace
J=P^{a}{}_{a}
its trace, and by
Y_{abc}=2\nabla_{[b}P_{c]a}
the Cotton tensor.
Note the Weyl tensor is independent of the choice of the metric from
[g]
.
Note
(M,[g])
is locally flat for
n\geq 4
if and only if
W_{abcd}=0
and, for
n=3
, if and only if
Y_{abc}=0
.

3.
Conformal-to-Einstein operator

We study the system of PDEs given by the 2nd order linear differential operator

(3)

E=E_{0}:\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],\quad\sigma\mapsto(\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b)_{0}}+P_{(ab)_{0}})\sigma

where the subscript
0
indicates the trace-free part with respect to

\bm{g}_{ab}
. This operator is conformally invariant and its non-vanishing
solutions provide rescaling to Einstein metrics in
[g]
. That is,
[g]
contains an Einstein metric
iff
E
has at least one solution (without zeros).

Our aim is to construct a compatibility complex for (
3
).
This seems to be difficult in general so we shall consider
only two special cases (which are rather opposite): the locally flat case and the ‘generic case’.
In the former case, the well known BGG resolution
provides the compatibility complex (cf. Section
4.3
). Our main interest is the generic case;
this is made precise in Section
3.2
.

3.1.
Conformally flat case.

The operator
E_{0}
is the first in the sequence of conformally invariant operators
E_{0},\ldots,E_{n-1}
where

E_{n-1}

is the formal adjoint of
E_{0}
and remaining operators are of the first order,

(4)

\displaystyle E_{k}:\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{b}[1]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{E}_{[a_{0}\ldots a_{k}]}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{b}[1],

\displaystyle\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}b}

\displaystyle\mapsto(k+1)\Pr{}_{\boxtimes}\nabla_{[a_{0}}\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]b}

for
1\leq k\leq n-2
. Here
\boxtimes
denotes the Cartan product, i.e., the bundle

\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{b}
is given by the Young tableaux
with one column of the length
k
and second column of the length
1

where we also project to the trace-free part with respect to
\bm{g}_{ab}
;

\Pr{}_{\boxtimes}
is exactly the projection to that bundle.
In particular,
\mathcal{E}_{a}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{b}=\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}
.

This sequence can be obtained form the exterior derivative twisted with the so called tractor connection
– which is flat in the locally flat case – via technique known as ‘BGG machinery’.
Assuming the locally flat setting, the tractor connection is flat hence and the sequence is known as the

BGG complex
, which is then known to be a compatibility complex in
the sense of Definition
4.2
.
We refer to Section
4.3
for details.

3.2.
Generic conformal structures.

Assuming
n\geq 4
, we shall call the conformal class
(M,[g])

generic
1
1

1

Called
weakly generic
in
[
17
, Sec.2.5]
.
if the Weyl tensor is injective as the map

(5)

W_{abcd}\colon\mathcal{E}^{d}\to\mathcal{E}_{abc}[2],\quad v^{d}\mapsto W_{abcd}v^{d}.

Then there exists an inversion
\overline{W}{}^{abce}\in\mathcal{E}^{[ab]ce}[-2]
,

\overline{W}{}^{abce}:\mathcal{E}_{abc}[2]\to\mathcal{E}^{e}\quad\text{such that}\quad\overline{W}{}^{abce}W_{abcd}=\delta_{d}^{e}.

Note such an inversion is not given uniquely. In that case, a specific
choice would have to be used as an extra geometric input to the rest of
our discussion. However, sometimes, there is a preferred choice. For
instance
[
17
, Sec.2.5]
, if the tensor

(6)

V_{a}^{b}:=W_{cdea}W^{cdeb}

is invertible as an endomorphism (equivalently
\det V\neq 0
), with
inverse
\overline{V}{}_{a}^{b}V_{b}^{c}=\delta_{a}^{c}
, we can use the preferred choice

(7)

\overline{W}{}^{abcd}:=W^{abce}\overline{V}{}_{e}^{d}.

The genericity assumption leads
[
17
]
to explicit curvature obstructions for existence of solutions
of
E_{0}
which we now briefly summarize. Using the operator
E_{1}
from Section
3.1
, explicitly
given by
E_{1}(\tau)_{abc}=2\Pr_{\boxtimes}(\nabla_{[a}\tau_{b]c})
for
\tau_{ab}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]
, we compute

(8)

F_{abc}:=(E_{1}\circ E_{0}(\sigma))_{abc}=W_{abcd}(\nabla^{d}\sigma)+Y_{cab}\sigma\in\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{c}\bigr)[1].

Applying the inverse
\overline{W}{}^{abce}
to (
8
), we obtain

(9)

\nabla_{a}\sigma+Z_{a}\sigma=\overline{W}{}^{rst}{}_{a}F_{rst}\quad\text{for}\quad Z_{a}:=\overline{W}{}^{rst}{}_{a}Y_{trs}\in\mathcal{E}_{a}.

Note the 1-form
Z
– determined by the choice of the inversion
\overline{W}{}^{abcd}
– is not conformally invariant.
If we change the metric to
\hat{g}_{ab}=e^{2\Upsilon}g_{ab}
, the Cotton tensor transforms as

\widehat{Y}_{bcd}=Y_{bcd}+\Upsilon^{a}W_{abcd}
where
\Upsilon_{a}=\nabla_{a}\Upsilon
. Therefore

(10)

\widehat{Z}_{a}=Z_{a}-\Upsilon_{a}.

Previous observation give rise to curvature obstructions for existence of solutions of
E_{0}
.
If
\sigma
is a solution then
\nabla_{a}\sigma+Z_{a}\sigma=0
. If we differentiate this relation once more, project
either to symmetric or skew-symmetric part and use
E_{0}(\sigma)=0
then

(11)

(dZ)_{ab}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi(Z)_{ab}=0

where

\displaystyle(dZ)_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}Z_{b]}\in\mathcal{E}_{[ab]},

\displaystyle\Phi(Z)_{ab}:=\nabla_{(a}Z_{b)_{0}}-\operatorname{P}_{(ab)_{0}}-Z_{(a}Z_{b)_{0}}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}.

In fact, one can easily see that
E_{0}
has a solution if and only if (
11
) holds.
In particular, the genericity assumption means the solution space of
E_{0}

is at most 1-dimensional. Finally note that both
(dZ)_{ab}
and
\Phi(Z)_{ab}
are conformally invariant
for the scale-dependent 1-form
Z_{a}
satisfying (
10
). This is easy to verify
by a direct computation. We refer to
[
17
]
for a more detailed presentation.

3.3.
Invariant differential operators

Invariant differential operators which are also natural –
in the sense they depend only on the conformal class
[g]
and not other choices – are fully classified
in the locally flat setting
[
5
]
. Existence of their
curved analogous is more involved but clearly operators
E_{i}
from Section
3.1
are well defined
on any conformal structure.

We shall need more operators which will be defined using the 1-form

Z
from (
9
). These operators will be conformally invariant but not natural as they are defined
only for generic conformal classes and also depend on the choice on inversion
\overline{W}{}^{abcd}
.
First observe that

(12)

\widetilde{\nabla}_{a}:\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],\quad\widetilde{\nabla}_{a}\sigma=\nabla_{a}\sigma+Z_{a}\sigma

for
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}[1]
, is a conformally invariant connection on
\mathcal{E}[1]
.
(Note that
\widetilde{\nabla}
is called the
\mathcal{C}
-connection in
[
15
]
.)
Indeed, it follows from (
2
) that
\nabla_{a}\sigma
transforms
to
\nabla_{a}\sigma+\Upsilon_{a}\sigma
in another metric
e^{2\Upsilon}g_{ab}
,
\Upsilon_{a}=\nabla_{a}\Upsilon
and
Z_{a}

transforms to
Z_{a}-\Upsilon_{a}
according to (
10
).
Note the curvature of
\widetilde{\nabla}_{a}
is
(dZ)_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}Z_{b]}
. Further, we shall denote
exterior derivative twisted by
\widetilde{\nabla}_{a}
by
\tilde{d}:=d^{\widetilde{\nabla}}
,

(13)

\begin{split}\tilde{d}:\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}[1]&\to\mathcal{E}_{[a_{0}\ldots a_{k}]}[1],\\
\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}&\mapsto(k+1)\bigl(\nabla_{[a_{0}}\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}+Z_{[a_{0}}\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}\bigr)\end{split}

for
\tau_{a_{1}\ldots a_{k}}\in\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\ldots a_{k}]}[1]
and
k\geq 0
.
By construction,
\tilde{d}
is conformally invariant.

Next, we shall need the operator

(14)

\begin{split}D_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{b}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],\quad\tau_{b}\mapsto\nabla_{(a}\tau_{b)_{0}}-Z_{(a}\tau_{b)_{0}}\end{split}

for
\tau_{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{b}[1]
.
Using a similar computation as above, one easily verifies conformal invariance of
D_{1}
.
The role of the subscript in this notation will be clarified later.
Further, we denote by
C_{1}
the composition of
E_{1}
with the inversion
\overline{W}{}^{abcd}
,

(15)

\begin{split}C_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],\quad\tau_{ab}\mapsto 2\overline{W}{}^{rst}{}_{a}\nabla_{[r}\tau_{s]t}\end{split}

for
\tau_{ab}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]
. This operator is invariant by construction.

Operators
D_{1}
and
C_{1}
interact nicely with
E_{0}
and
\tilde{d}
. Specifically

(16)

\begin{split}D_{1}\circ\tilde{d}&=E_{0}+\Phi(Z):\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],\\
C_{1}\circ E_{0}&=\tilde{d}:\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],\end{split}

where we consider
\Phi(Z)
as an algebraic operator.
This follows from (
9
), (
11
) and a direct computation. Using these
operators, we can also recover obstructions from (
11
),

(17)

\begin{split}&(\tilde{d}\circ C_{1}\circ E_{0})(\sigma)=(dZ)\sigma,\\
&(D_{1}\circ C_{1}\circ E_{0})(\sigma)=\Phi(Z)\sigma+E_{0}(\sigma).\end{split}

We can also compose operators
C_{1}
and
D_{1}
. It turns out this leads
to an auxiliary operator that can be considered as an analogue of
D_{1}
where the source bundle is
replaced by
\mathcal{E}_{[bc]}[1]
and the target bundle by
\mathcal{E}_{a}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{bc}[1]\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{a[bc]}[1]
.
Recall
\boxtimes
is the Cartan product, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{a}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{bc}[1]
obeys symmetries of the Young diagram
with one column of the length
2
and the second column of the length

1
, and is traceless with respect to
\bm{g}_{ab}
. This auxiliary operator is

(18)

\begin{split}\overline{D}:\mathcal{E}_{[bc]}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{[bc]}[1],\quad\tau_{bc}\mapsto\Pr{}_{\boxtimes}(\nabla_{a}-2Z_{a})\tau_{bc}\end{split}

for
\tau_{bc}\in\mathcal{E}_{[bc]}[1]
. Here we use the notation
\Pr_{\boxtimes}
in the same way as in
Section
3.1
. A standard computation verifies
\overline{D}
is conformally invariant.

Now a direct computation verifies that

\displaystyle C_{1}\!\circ\!D_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{a}[1]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],

(19)

\displaystyle\tau_{a}

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau_{a}-(H^{\prime}_{1}\tilde{d}\tau)_{a}-\overline{W}{}^{rsp}{}_{a}\bigl[2\Phi(Z)_{pr}\tau_{s}+\tfrac{1}{2}(dZ)_{rs}\tau_{p}\bigr],

\displaystyle\text{with}~H^{\prime}_{1}:\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}[1]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],

(20)

\displaystyle\psi_{ab}

\displaystyle\mapsto-\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{W}{}^{rsp}{}_{a}\overline{D}(\psi)_{prs}.

Conformal invariance of
\overline{D}
means that also
H^{\prime}_{1}
is conformally invariant.

Finally note that all invariant operators
E_{i}
,
\widetilde{\nabla}
,
\tilde{d}
,
C_{1}
,
D_{1}
,
\overline{D}
,
H^{\prime}_{1}

are operators between bundles with all “indices downstairs” with the weight 1.
That is, these operators do not change the conformal weight.

4.
Compatibility operators

Below, in Section
4.1
we briefly recall the
precise definition of a compatibility complex
(Definition
4.2
). Then, in
Section
4.2
we recall the method given
in
[
19
]
of constructing a compatibility complex for a
differential operator that is uniformly of finite type
(Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
).
This method will later be used in the desired construction of a
compatibility complex for the conformal-to-Einstein operator

E_{0}
(
3
) (Section
5
) for ‘generic’ conformal
structures. Finally, in Section
4.3
we sketch how this
method can also establish that a BGG sequence (such as (
4
))
on a locally flat geometry constitutes a compatibility complex in the
sense defined here, which is an expected result that is sometimes taken
for granted, but is difficult to locate in the literature.

4.1.
Background

We start by introducing some basic notions from
homological
algebra

[
27
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

A (possibly infinite) composable sequence
K_{l}
of linear maps,

l=l_{\min},\ldots,l_{\max}
, such that

K_{l+1}\circ K_{l}=0
when possible, is called a
(cochain)
complex
. Given complexes
K_{l}
and
K^{\prime}_{l}
a sequence
C_{l}
of linear

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

such that its squares commute, that is
K^{\prime}_{l}\circ C_{l}=C_{l+1}\circ K_{l}
when possible, is called a
cochain map
or a
morphism

between complexes. A
homotopy
between complexes
K_{l}
and
K^{\prime}_{l}

(which could also be the same complex,
K_{l}=K^{\prime}_{l}
) is a sequence of
morphism, as the dashed arrows in the diagram

(22)

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{}{}{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 220.7 -35.04 C 248.07 -27.7 248.07 -51.46 221.23 -44.26}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.96591}{0.2589}{-0.2589}{-0.96591}{159.69255pt}{-31.9368pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{176.6899pt}{-31.04843pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

such that
C_{l}
and
D_{l}
are mutual inverses up to homotopy (
H_{l}
and

H^{\prime}_{l}
), that is

(24)

\displaystyle D_{l}\circ C_{l}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-K_{l-1}\circ H_{l-1}-H_{l}\circ K_{l},

\displaystyle C_{l}\circ D_{l}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-K^{\prime}_{l-1}\circ H^{\prime}_{l-1}-H^{\prime}_{l}\circ K^{\prime}_{l},

with the special end cases

(25)

\displaystyle D_{l_{\min}}\circ C_{l_{\min}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-\tilde{H}_{l_{\min}-1}-H_{l_{\min}}\circ K_{l_{\min}},

\displaystyle K_{l_{\min}}\circ\tilde{H}_{l_{\min}-1}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle C_{l_{\min}}\circ D_{l_{\min}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{l_{\min}-1}-H^{\prime}_{l_{\min}}\circ K^{\prime}_{l_{\min}},

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{l_{\min}}\circ\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{l_{\min}-1}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle D_{l_{\max}+1}\circ C_{l_{\max}+1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-H_{l_{\max}}\circ K_{l_{\max}}-\tilde{H}_{l_{\max}+1},

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{l_{\max}+1}\circ K_{l_{\max}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle C_{l_{\max}+1}\circ D_{l_{\max}+1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}-H^{\prime}_{l_{\max}}\circ K^{\prime}_{l_{\max}}-\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{l_{\max}+1},

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{l_{\max}+1}\circ K^{\prime}_{l_{\max}}

\displaystyle=0,

where the
\tilde{H}
maps are allowed to be arbitrary, as long as they
satisfy the given identities. The
\tilde{H}
operators may be omitted from
the data, since they can be defined to satisfy to the left column
identities in (
25
), as long as they satisfy the
identities in the left column of (
25
).

Note that our definition of equivalence up to homotopy between complexes
of finite length is set up in a way that allows an equivalence between
longer complexes to be truncated and still remain an equivalence.

Next, we restrict our attention to the case where all maps are given by
differential operators.

Definition 4.2

(cf.
[
22
, Def.10.5.4]
,
[
24
, Defs.1.2.2–4]
)
.

Given a differential operator
K
, any composable differential operator

L
such that
L\circ K=0
is a
(partial) compatibility
operator
for
K
. If
K_{1}
is a compatibility operator for
K
, it is
called
complete
or
universal
when any other compatibility
operator
L
can be factored through
L=L^{\prime}\circ K_{1}
for some
differential operator
L^{\prime}
. A complex of differential operators
K_{l}
,

l=0,1,\ldots
is called a compatibility complex for
K
when
K_{0}=K

and, for each
l\geq 1
,
K_{l}
is a complete compatibility operator for

K_{l-1}
.

Our usage of attributes like
partial
,
complete
or

universal
is not standard. In the literature, when no attribute
is used, the completeness condition is
implied
[
24
, Def.1.2.2]
, while a partial compatibility
operator does not seem to have a standard name.

4.2.
Sufficient conditions and construction

We can make two related remarks about the properties of compatibility operators.
A complete compatibility operator, say
K_{1}
, need not be
unique. But, by its universal factorization property, any two
compatibility operators, say
K_{1}
and
K^{\prime}_{1}
, must factor through each
other,
K_{1}=L_{1}\circ K^{\prime}_{1}
and
K^{\prime}_{1}=L^{\prime}_{1}\circ K_{1}
for some
differential operators
L_{1}
and
L^{\prime}_{1}
. On the other hand, while the
compatibility condition
K_{1}\circ K=0
is easy to check, it may be
quite challenging to verify the completeness/universality property of
any particular
K_{1}
. However, of both
K
and
K_{1}
may be reduced to
some other pair of operators, say
K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}_{1}
, where the
compatibility and completeness conditions are known to be satisfied,
this reduction may serve as a witness to the completeness property of

K_{1}
. Both of these remarks motivate the utility of the following

Proposition 4.3

(
[
19
, Lem.4]
)
.

Consider two complexes of differential operators
K_{l}
and
K_{l}^{\prime}
, for

l=0,1,\cdots,n-1
, and an equivalence up to homotopy between them, as
in the diagram

(26)

where for simplicity we are assuming that the
\tilde{H}_{-1}
,

\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{-1}
,
\tilde{H}_{n}
,
\tilde{H}^{\prime}_{n}
are all zero.
If
K^{\prime}_{l}
is a compatibility complex for
K^{\prime}_{0}
, then
K_{l}
is
a compatibility complex for
K_{0}
.

The observation that is at the core of our method of explicitly
constructing compatibility operators, together with a witness to their
completeness property is the following

Proposition 4.4

(cf.
[
19
, Lem.5]
,
[
24
, Prp.1.2.7]
)
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

If its truncation to the first square is an equivalence up to homotopy
in the sense of Definition
4.1
(of length 1) and the
operator
K^{\prime}_{1}
is known to be a complete compatibility operator for

K^{\prime}
, then there exist differential operators
H^{\prime}_{1}
and
D^{\prime}_{2}
such
that the second square in the above diagram completes it to an
equivalence up to homotopy (of length 2).

A consequence of this observation is that, by
Proposition
4.3
the operator
K_{1}

in (
27
) is witnessed to be also be a complete
compatibility operator for
K
. In practice, this
K_{1}
operator can
contain quite a lot of redundant information, so some simplification by
hand may be required to bring it into a more convenient form. As long as
the simplifications keep track of the witnessing equivalence data, then
the simplified
K_{1}
is still easily verified to be complete.

4.3.
BGG sequence as a compatibility complex

Here we sketch an argument for the result that
BGG complexes provide compatibility complexes
(of first BGG operators) on locally flat parabolic geometries.
Here we sketch how this follows from Proposition
4.3
.
A version of this argument appears in
[
12
, Sec.4]
, in the
specific case of the Calabi complex, the BGG sequence for the Killing
operator.
We assume references
[
25
,
9
]
as background material for this section.

A BGG complex is parametrized by choice a tractor bundle
\mathcal{V}
. Some
representation theoretic data then builds its operators, which we can
generically call
E_{l}
, and supplies all the data that produces an
instance of an equivalence up to homotopy, as illustrated
in (
26
), with a reference compatibility complex. We
illustrate a generic square of the resulting diagram, which can be
referred to in the rest of the discussion:

(28)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The lower level of (
26
)
is given by bundles
V_{l}^{\prime}:=\Lambda^{l}T^{*}M\otimes\mathcal{V}
together with

K_{l}^{\prime}=d^{\overline{\nabla}}:\Gamma(V_{l}^{\prime})\to\Gamma(V_{l+1}^{\prime})
where
\overline{\nabla}

is the flat tractor connection on
\mathcal{V}
.

Lie algebraic data for parabolic geometries give rise to the Kostant’s codifferential and its bundle version

\partial^{*}:V_{l}^{\prime}\to V_{l-1}^{\prime}
. We put
V_{l}:=\ker\partial^{*}/\operatorname{im}\partial^{*}
in
V_{l}^{\prime}
.
These are the bundles in the upper level of (
26
).
By construction, there is a projection
\pi_{l}:\ker\partial^{*}|_{V_{l}^{\prime}}\to V_{l}
and, moreover, there is
a differential splitting
\Pi_{l}:\Gamma(V_{l})\to\Gamma(\ker\partial^{*}|_{V_{l}^{\prime}})
which is uniquely
characterized by conditions
\partial^{*}\Pi_{l}=0
,
\partial^{*}d^{\overline{\nabla}}\Pi_{l}=0
and

\pi_{l}\circ\Pi_{l}=\mathrm{id}_{V_{l}}
. The interaction of these tools gives rise to the calculus known
as “BGG machinery”
[
25
,
9
]
.
In particular, this yields BGG operators
K_{l}=E_{l}
in the upper level of (
26
) in the form

E_{l}=\pi_{l+1}d^{\overline{\nabla}}\Pi_{l}
.

The last ingredient we need is the differential operator

\Box=\partial^{*}d^{\overline{\nabla}}+d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*}:\Gamma(V_{l}^{\prime})\to\Gamma(V_{l}^{\prime})
.
Note that
\Box
reduces to
\partial^{*}d^{\nabla}
on sections of
\operatorname{im}\partial^{*}
and to

d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*}
on sections of associated graded bundle of
V_{l}^{\prime}/\ker\partial^{*}
.
Since
\Box
is closely related
[
9
,
26
]
to the so called Kostant’s Laplacian
[
20
]
,
it follows from representation theory that we have polynomials

P_{l}
and
P^{\prime}_{l}
, both with constant term equal to
1
, such that corresponding polynomial operators satisfy

\displaystyle P_{l}(\partial^{*}d^{\nabla})|_{\Gamma(\operatorname{im}\partial^{*})}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*}):\Gamma(V_{l}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\Gamma(\ker\partial^{*}),

\displaystyle P_{l}(\partial^{*}d^{\nabla})P^{\prime}_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})|_{\operatorname{im}\Pi}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id},

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{l}

\displaystyle=P_{l-1}.

Now we can specify all missing operators in (
26
). We put
C_{l}:=\Pi_{l}
,

D_{l}:=\pi_{l}P^{\prime}_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})
and
H_{l}=0
. Further, using polynomials

Q_{l}(x)
given by

P_{l}(x)=xQ_{l}(x)+1
and properties in the previous display, we have

\displaystyle P_{l}(\partial^{*}d^{\overline{\nabla}})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})=\bigl(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*}Q_{l-1}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})+\mathrm{id}\bigr)\bigl(Q_{l}(\partial^{*}d^{\overline{\nabla}})\partial^{*}d^{\overline{\nabla}}+\mathrm{id}\bigr)=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}+d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*}Q_{l-1}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})+\partial^{*}Q_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})d^{\overline{\nabla}}=C_{l}D_{l}.

Now we put
H^{\prime}_{l}=-\partial^{*}Q_{l}(d^{\overline{\nabla}}\partial^{*})
and
the identities (
24
) are satisfied, which verifies equivalence of the BGG complex
E_{l}
and
the twisted de-Rham complex
d^{\overline{\nabla}}
are equivalent up to homotopy, hence
E_{l}
is a compatibility complex
for
E_{0}
.

5.
Compatibility complex for C2E operator

Assume the conformal structure
(M,[g_{ab}])
of dimension
n\geq 4
is generic in the sense of (
5
).
We shall comment upon the dimension
3
in Remark
5.3
.
Then the solution space of
E(\sigma)=0
has either dimension
1
or
0
. We shall discuss these two cases separately.
Recall 1-dimensional solution space is characterized by the property (
11
), see
Section
3.2
.

5.1.
No non-trivial solution

Following (
11
), this case is characterized by

\begin{pmatrix}(dZ)_{ab}\\
\Phi(Z)_{ab}\end{pmatrix}\neq 0,\quad\text{equivalently}\quad\begin{pmatrix}\zeta^{ab}&\eta^{ab}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(dZ)_{ab}\\
\Phi(Z)_{ab}\end{pmatrix}=1,

for some tensor fields
\zeta^{ab}\in\mathcal{E}^{[ab]}
and
\eta^{ab}\in\mathcal{E}^{(ab)_{0}}
.
Then there exists a left inverse
H_{0}:\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]\to\mathcal{E}[1]
of
E=E_{0}
,

(29)

\displaystyle H_{0}\circ E_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{E}[1]},\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle H_{0}(\tau):=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta^{ab}&\eta^{ab}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(\tilde{d}\circ C_{1}(\tau))_{ab}\\
((D_{1}\circ C_{1}-\mathrm{id})(\tau))_{ab}\end{pmatrix}

for
\tau_{ab}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]
, cf. (
17
).

If we blindly apply the successive construction of the compatibility
operators from Proposition
4.4
, we end up with the
following equivalence diagram, which repeats the last two squares
indefinitely and hence has infinite length:

(30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet\hbox to0.0pt{ $\cdots$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\hbox to0.0pt{ $\cdots$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]}-E_{0}H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

However, it is easy to notice that the second compatibility operator

E_{0}H_{0}
contains many redundant components, since by
using (
29
) we can factor

H_{0}=H_{0}(E_{0}H_{0})=(H_{0}E_{0})H_{0}.

It is straightforward to rewrite the third equivalence square
in (
30
) using
H_{0}
instead of
E_{0}H_{0}
.
Then, continuing to apply the construction from
Proposition (
4.4
) we arrive at a much simplified
equivalence diagram, now of length 3:

(31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}-E_{0}H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

We can encapsulate the result of this calculation in the following rather generic

Lemma 5.1
.

Given a differential operator
E_{0}
, provided there exists a
left-inverse differential operator
H_{0}
, such that

(32)

H_{0}\circ E_{0}=\mathrm{id},

then the top line of (
31
) is a full
compatibility complex for
E_{0}
of length 3.

Proof.

The identity (
32
) is sufficient to verify that the data
in diagram (
31
) constitutes an equivalence of
complexes up to homotopy, with the bottom line trivially being a
compatibility complex. Therefore, by
Proposition
4.3
, the top line is also a
compatibility complex. By inspection, the construction of
Proposition
4.4
yields
0
as the next compatibility
operator. Hence the diagram can be truncated at length 3.
∎

5.2.
One dimensional solution space

This case is characterized by
dZ=0
and
\Phi(Z)=0
, see (
11
) and discussion below.
We assume
n\geq 4
as a one dimensional solution space is not possible for
n=3
. In the latter dimension,
existence of an Einstein metric in
[g_{ab}]
means the Cotton tensor is zero hence
[g_{ab}]
is conformally flat.

It turns out, we shall need a rather specific case of Proposition
4.4
here:
it is sufficient to assume
C_{0}=D_{0}=\mathrm{id}
and
H_{0}=H_{0}^{\prime}=0
in the first square of (
27
). Then
one can follow Proposition
4.4
to obtain a compatibility complex. However – similar to
Section
5.1
– this can be heavily simplified. Using a small number of key identities, we obtain
the following general result:

Lemma 5.2
.

Consider the following diagram of differential operators:

(33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{smallmatrix}V_{1}\\
\oplus\\
V^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{smallmatrix}V^{\prime}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
V^{\prime}_{3}\end{smallmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{4}~\hbox to0.0pt{$\cdots$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{4}~\hbox to0.0pt{$\cdots$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathrm{id}-D_{1}C_{1}\\
K^{\prime}_{1}C_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&-H^{\prime}_{1}\\
0&K^{\prime}_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathrm{id}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathrm{id}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&K^{\prime}_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}&0\\
-K^{\prime}_{1}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\mathrm{id}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}H^{\prime}_{1}\\
\mathrm{id}-K^{\prime}_{1}H^{\prime}_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\mathrm{id}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

continued to the right by
C_{i}=D_{i}=\mathrm{id}
and
H_{i}=H^{\prime}_{i}=0
.
Provided that the bottom row is a full compatibility complex, its
truncation to the first cell is an equivalence up to homotopy and
moreover
H^{\prime}_{1}
is an operator satisfying the identity

(34)

\mathrm{id}-C_{1}\circ D_{1}=H^{\prime}_{1}\circ K_{1}^{\prime},

then the diagram in (
33
) is an equivalence up to
homotopy and its top row is a full compatibility complex.

Proof.

All the required identities can be checked by explicit calculation,
reducing them to the identities in the hypotheses. The conclusion
follows from a simple application of Proposition
4.4
.
∎

An immediate consequence is our main Theorem
1.1
, which exhibits
a compatibility complex for the conformal-to-Einstein operator

E_{0}
(
3
) with one independent solution, with a generic Weyl tensor.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The complex from the statement of the theorem is exactly the top line
of (
33
), provided that

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}[1],

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{(ab)_{0}}[1],

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\cdots a_{i}]}[1],

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=E_{0},

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{d},

and operators
C_{1}
and
D_{1}
are given by (
15
) and (
14
),
with
H^{\prime}_{1}
given by (
20
). So the conclusion that it is a
compatibility complex follows from Lemma
5.2
. We need only
check the commutativity of the first square of (
33
),
which is ensured by (
16
), and the identity (
34
)
involving
H^{\prime}_{1}
, which is just a rearrangement of
identity (
19
).
∎

Remark 5.3
.

It turns out that our construction of compatibility complexes provides a complete answer
in certain low dimensional cases.

(1) Assume dimension
n=3
. Then the Weyl tensor is identically zero for all conformal structures
(M,[g])
.
We need to use the Cotton tensor
Y_{abc}
instead:
Y_{abc}
vanishes if and only if
(M,[g])

is conformally flat. Moreover, the solution space of
E_{0}
is trivial unless
(M,[g])
is flat.
Thus the suitable notion of genericity is the assumption
Y_{abc}\not=0
everywhere.
Then using
\zeta^{abc}Y_{abc}=1
for some
\zeta^{abc}
, we have
\zeta^{abc}(E_{1}\circ E_{0}(\sigma))_{abc}=\sigma

according to (
8
). This gives rise to
H_{0}
satisfying
H_{0}\circ E_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{E}[1]}

and one can follow Section
5.1
to construct a compatibility complex (of the length 3).

(2) Assuming
n=4
, there is the well-known dimension-dependent identity
[
14
, Eq.(31)]

(35)

W^{abcd}W_{abce}=\tfrac{1}{4}|W|^{2}\delta_{d}^{e}.\quad|W|^{2}=W^{abcd}W_{abcd}.

Thus assuming moreover the Riemannian signature, the invertibility discussion at (
6
)
and (
7
) shows that the Weyl tensor
is either generic or identically zero. Further, one easily verifies that two linearly independent solutions

\sigma,\bar{\sigma}\in\mathcal{E}[1]
of
E_{0}
give rise to the conformal Killing field

k_{a}=\sigma\nabla_{a}\bar{\sigma}-\bar{\sigma}\nabla_{a}\sigma
such that
W_{abcd}k^{d}=0
(in any dimension and signature),
cf.
[
16
]
;
this means non-genericity – therefore conformal flatness (
W=0
) – in dimension 4. Summarizing,
we have the compatibility complex for all 4-dimensional conformal structures in the Riemannian signature.

(3) One can consider similar identities as in (
35
) with more complicated tensor fields.
Assume
n=4
and consider the following polynomial tensor in
W_{abcd}
,

\overset{2}{W}_{abcd}:=W_{rsab}W^{rs}{}_{cd}\quad\text{and}\quad\overset{3}{V}{}_{a}^{b}:=W_{rs}{}^{tb}W^{rs}{}_{pq}W^{pq}{}_{ta}.

If
|W|^{2}=0
then also
W_{rsta}W^{rstb}=0
according to (
35
) hence

\overset{2}{W}_{abcd}
is totally trace-free. Following the approach of
[
14
]
,
this means the vanishing of
\overset{2}{W}_{[ab}{}^{[cd}\delta_{e]}{}^{f]}=0
. Contracting this relation
with
W^{ab}{}_{cd}
yields

\overset{3}{V}{}_{a}^{b}=\tfrac{1}{4}\overset{3}{V}{}_{r}^{r}\delta_{a}^{b}.

For non-definite signatures, this can be used instead of (
35
) in cases when
|W|^{2}=0
but

\overset{3}{V}{}_{r}^{r}
is nonzero everywhere.

Neither of inversions of the Weyl tensor mentioned above works for all generic 4-manifolds.
In Appendix
A
we exhibit a
family of examples in Lorentzian signature in 4-dimensions where
any

scalar covariantly formed from the Weyl tensor must vanish. These are
Petrov Type III Weyl tensors in the terminology of Appendix
A
.

6.
A projective analogue

There is a close analogue of the conformal operator
E_{0}
in projective geometry.
In fact, also the compatibility complex has a close projective relative. To show that,
we need to find projective versions of invariant operators discussed in Section
3
;
the construction in Section
5
will be just the same (using projective
versions of all invariant operators needed there).

Here we follow the notation from
[
3
]
.
The projective structure
(M,[\nabla])
on a smooth orientable manifold
M
,
\dim M\geq 2
is given by a class
\nabla
of
special projectively equivalent torsion free connections. That is, connections in
[\nabla]

are volume preserving and have
the same class of geodesics (as unparametrized curves).
Then the difference between
\nabla,\widehat{\nabla}\in[\nabla]
is controlled
by an exact 1-form
\Upsilon_{a}=\nabla_{a}\Upsilon
,
\Upsilon\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

(36)

\begin{split}&\hat{\nabla}_{a}\sigma=\nabla_{a}\sigma+w\Upsilon_{a}\sigma,\\
&\hat{\nabla}_{a}\tau_{b}=\nabla_{a}\tau_{b}-\Upsilon_{a}\tau_{b}-\Upsilon_{b}\tau_{a},\\
&\hat{\nabla}_{a}\varphi^{b}=\nabla_{a}\varphi^{b}+\Upsilon_{a}\varphi^{b}+\Upsilon_{r}\varphi^{r}\delta_{a}^{b}\end{split}

where
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}[w]
,
\tau_{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{b}
and
\varphi^{b}\in\mathcal{E}^{b}
. Here
\mathcal{E}[w]
are projective density
bundles, see
[
3
]
for details. In this convention we have
\Lambda^{n}T^{*}M\cong\mathcal{E}[-(n+1)]
.
The Ricci tensor
Ric_{ab}
and projective Schouten tensor
\operatorname{P}_{ab}
are related by

Ric_{ab}=(n-1)\operatorname{P}_{ab}
and these are symmetric tensors (for special connections
\nabla
).
The projective Cotton tensor is given by
Y_{abc}=2\nabla_{[b}\operatorname{P}_{c]a}
.
The projectively invariant Weyl tensor
W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}
is the trace free part of the curvature tensor

R_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}
of
\nabla
. Projective flatness is characterized by
W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}=0
for
n\geq 3
and
by
Y_{abc}=0
for
n=2
.

We start with the sequence of operators
E_{i}
given by formula (
4
)
which we consider in the projective sense:
\mathcal{E}[1]
are projective densities and the meaning
of the Cartan product is slightly different as
\boxtimes
denotes only suitable symmetrizations of indices
(there is no trace to be removed when all indices are down).
This sequence of operators is projectively invariant and
it is a complex for projective flat class
[\nabla]
. Following the discussion in Section
4.3
, it
provides a compatibility complex of the
projective-to-Ricci-flat
operator

(37)

E_{0}\colon\mathcal{E}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)},\quad\sigma\mapsto(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}+\operatorname{P}_{ab})\sigma.

That is, solutions
\sigma
of
E_{0}
without zeros correspond to Ricci-flat connections
\widehat{\nabla}
in
[\nabla]
.
Specifically, if
\nabla
corresponds to the volume form
\epsilon
(given uniquely up to a constant multiple),
i.e.
\nabla\epsilon=0
, and we identify
\sigma
with a function using
\epsilon
, then

\hat{\epsilon}=\sigma^{-(n+1)}\epsilon
corresponds to
\widehat{\nabla}
.

Next we discuss a suitable genericity condition for our purpose.
Assuming projective meaning, we have

(38)

F_{abc}:=(E_{1}\circ E_{0}(\sigma))_{abd}=-W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}(\nabla_{c}\sigma)+Y_{dab}\sigma\in\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{c}\bigr)[1],

differs from (
8
) by position of indices of the Weyl tensor (which we cannot raise or lower).
Thus we shall call
(M,[\nabla])
generic if the bundle map
\tau_{c}\mapsto W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}\tau_{c}
is injective.
Then there is an inverse
\overline{W}{}^{ab}{}_{e}{}^{d}
of this map, i.e.,
\overline{W}{}^{ab}{}_{e}{}^{d}W_{ab}{}^{c}{}_{d}=\delta_{e}^{c}
.
Applying the inverse
-\overline{W}{}^{ab}{}_{e}{}^{d}
to the previous display, we obtain

(39)

\nabla_{a}\sigma+Z_{a}\sigma=-\overline{W}{}^{rs}{}_{a}{}^{t}F_{rst}\quad\text{for}\quad Z_{a}:=-\overline{W}{}^{rs}{}_{a}{}^{t}Y_{trs}\in\mathcal{E}_{a}.

By direct computation, we have
\widehat{Z}_{a}=Z_{a}-\Upsilon_{a}
under the projective transformation (
36
).
Further, a similar computation as in Section
3.3
shows that the projective equation
E_{0}(\sigma)=0

has a nontrivial solution if and only if
(dZ)_{ab}=0
and
\Phi(Z)_{ab}=0
where

\displaystyle(dZ)_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}Z_{b]}\in\mathcal{E}_{[ab]},

\displaystyle\Phi(Z)_{ab}:=\nabla_{(a}Z_{b)}-\operatorname{P}_{(ab)}-Z_{(a}Z_{b)}\in\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}.

Next we go through conformally invariant operators in Section
3.3
and discuss their projective analogues. First, (exterior) covariant derivatives
\widetilde{\nabla}
and
\tilde{d}
are given exactly as in (
12
) and (
13
). Projectively invariant operators

C_{1}
and
D_{1}
can be obtained by a slight modification of (
15
) and (
14
), namely

(40)

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{a}[1],

\displaystyle\tau_{ab}

\displaystyle\mapsto-2\overline{W}{}^{rs}{}_{a}{}^{t}\nabla_{[r}\tau_{s]t},

(41)

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{E}_{b}[1]\to\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}[1],

\displaystyle\tau_{b}

\displaystyle\mapsto\nabla_{(a}\tau_{b)}-Z_{(a}\tau_{b)}.

Now it is easy to verify relations summarized in (
16
) and (
17
)
hold also in the projective case. Moreover, the operator
\overline{D}
defined by (
18
)
is projectively invariant and we obtain also a projective modification of (
19
):

(42)

\displaystyle\bigl((C_{1}\circ D_{1})\tau\bigr)_{a}

\displaystyle=\tau_{a}-(H^{\prime}_{1}(\tilde{d}\tau))_{a}\!-\!\overline{W}{}^{rs}{}_{a}{}^{p}\bigl[2\Phi(Z)_{pr}\tau_{s}+\tfrac{1}{2}(dZ)_{rs}\tau_{p}\bigr],

where

(43)

\displaystyle(H^{\prime}_{1}\psi)_{a}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{W}{}^{rs}{}_{a}{}^{p}(\overline{D}\psi)_{prs},

for
\tau_{a}\in\mathcal{E}_{a}[1]
,
\psi_{ab}\in\mathcal{E}_{[ab]}[1]
.
Projective invariance of
\overline{D}
means that also
H^{\prime}_{1}
is projectively invariant.

Theorem 6.1
.

In dimension
n\geq 3
, for the projective-to-Ricci-flat operator

E_{0}
(
37
) of a generic projective class
[\nabla]
,
its compatibility complex is given by the top line
of (
33
), provided that

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}[1],

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{(ab)}[1],

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{[a_{1}\cdots a_{i}]}[1]

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=E_{0},

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\tilde{d}

and operators
C_{1}
and
D_{1}
are given by (
40
)
and (
41
), with
H^{\prime}_{1}
given by (
43
).

Proof.

Again, the conclusion follows from Lemma
5.2
. We need
only check the commutativity of the first square
of (
33
), which is ensured by the projective analog of
identities (
16
), and the identity (
34
)
involving
H^{\prime}_{1}
, which is now a rearrangement of
identity (
42
).
∎

For dimension
n=2
,
(M,[\nabla])
is either projectively flat or there is
only trivial solution of projective
E_{0}
. In the latter case, the
projective Cotton tensor
Y_{abc}
is nonzero and one can easily
reproduce the discussion in Remark
5.3
(1).

Appendix A
Lorentzian signature in 4-dimensions

We have noted in the text that the choice (
7
) of the
inversion
\overline{W}{}^{abcd}
, combined with the 4-dimensional
identity (
35
) in Remark
5.3
(2), works for any generic Weyl tensor of a Riemannian
metric. That is because the quadratic invariant
|W|^{2}=W_{abcd}W^{abcd}\neq 0
if
W_{abcd}\neq 0
. The last implication no longer
holds for indefinite signature metrics. But does then the
inversion (
7
) necessarily fail? Remark
5.3
(3) shows that more complicated canonical inversions (where
\overline{W}{}^{abcd}
is covariantly and algebraically determined from
W_{abcd}
) may work when
|W|^{2}=0
. However, in this Appendix, we
find explicit examples in Lorentzian signature where all such canonical inversions fail.

When dealing with a 4-dimensional conformal structure
(M,[g])
, where
the signature of a metric representative
g
is Lorentzian

({-}{+}{+}{+})
, it is sometimes convenient to use an double null frame

\{l,n,m,\bar{m}\}
adapted to
g
, where
l,n
are real null vectors, while

m,\bar{m}
are complex conjugate null vectors, with the only non-vanishing
inner products
g(l,n)=1
and
g(m,\bar{m})=-1
. We are following the
conventions of
[
10
, §8]
. The structural properties of
this frame are preserved by the boosts in the
(l,n)
-plane and
rotations in the
m,\bar{m}
plane. As such we can assign the following
(boost, spin) weights to the frame vectors:
|l|=(1,0)
,
|n|=(-1,0)
,
|m|=(0,1)
,
|m|=(0,-1)
, which are then preserved by
tensor products, permutations and metric contractions. The boost and
spin weights naturally extend to the elements of the frame adapted basis
of any tensor space.

In 4-dimensions, the bundle
\mathcal{W}_{pqrs}
of 4-tensors with the same
structure as the Weyl tensor
W_{pqrs}
(the same permutation symmetries
as the Riemann tensor and traceless) has rank 10 and the fibers can be
conveniently parametrized by the following 5 complex

Newman-Penrose scalars

[
10
, Eq.(1.294)]

(44)

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=-W_{pqrs}l^{p}m^{q}l^{r}m^{s},

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle=-W_{pqrs}l^{p}n^{q}l^{r}m^{s},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle=-W_{pqrs}l^{p}m^{q}\bar{m}^{r}n^{s},

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle=-W_{pqrs}l^{p}n^{q}\bar{m}^{r}n^{s},

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}

\displaystyle=-W_{pqrs}n^{p}\bar{m}^{q}n^{r}\bar{m}^{s}.

They are chosen so that when only one of these scalars is non-zero, the
corresponding Weyl tensor has pure boost weight, namely weight
-2
for

\Psi_{0}
,
-1
for
\Psi_{1}
,
0
for
\Psi_{2}
,
1
for
\Psi_{3}
and
2

for
\Psi_{4}
. The
Petrov classification

[
10
, §9]

of the algebraic type of the Weyl tensor corresponds to the following
filtration: Type I (
\Psi_{0}=0
), Type II (
\Psi_{0}=\Psi_{1}=0
), Type III
(
\Psi_{0}=\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}=0
) and Type N (
\Psi_{0}=\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}=\Psi_{3}=0
).
The reconstruction of the original tensor from the
scalars (
44
) is given by
[
10
, Eq.(1.298)]
2
2

2

That formula has a typo; it is missing a factor of

\frac{1}{2}
in front of its first term.
, but in a somewhat awkward form. We give an alternative formula below,
which is more convenient for our purposes.

We will use the following symmetrized and
Kulkarni-Nomizu

products

(45)

\displaystyle(AB)_{pr}

\displaystyle=A_{(p}B_{r)},

(46)

\displaystyle(A\odot B)_{pqrs}

\displaystyle=A_{pr}B_{qs}-A_{qr}B_{ps}-A_{ps}B_{qr}+A_{qs}B_{pr}.

If
A,B\in\mathcal{E}_{(pr)}
, then
(A\odot B)_{pqrs}
has the same index permutation symmetries as a Weyl tensor, with the exception of not automatically being traceless.
A convenient reconstruction formula from the Newman-Penrose
scalars (
44
) is

(47)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=-\Psi_{0}(nn)\odot(\bar{m}\bar{m})-\Psi_{0}^{*}(nn)\odot(mm)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+2\Psi_{1}\big((nn)\odot(l\bar{m})+(nm)\odot(\bar{m}\bar{m})\big)+2\Psi_{1}^{*}\big((nn)\odot(lm)+(n\bar{m})\odot(mm)\big)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-(\Psi_{0}+\Psi_{0}^{*})\big((ll)\odot(nn)+(mm)\odot(\bar{m}\bar{m})-2(ln)\odot(m\bar{m})\big)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+2i(\Psi_{0}-\Psi_{0}^{*})\big((lm)\odot(n\bar{m})-(l\bar{m})\odot(nm)\big)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+2\Psi_{3}^{*}\big((ll)\odot(n\bar{m})+(lm)\odot(\bar{m}\bar{m})\big)+2\Psi_{3}\big((ll)\odot(nm)+(l\bar{m})\odot(mm)\big)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-\Psi_{4}^{*}(ll)\odot(\bar{m}\bar{m})-\Psi_{4}(ll)\odot(mm).

The quadratic invariant of the Weyl tensor evaluates to

(48)

|W|^{2}=W_{pqrs}W^{pqrs}=16\Re(\Psi_{0}\Psi_{4}-4\Psi_{1}\Psi_{3}+3\Psi_{2}^{2}).

Since the
\Psi_{i}
can be arbitrary complex numbers, we can definitely
find choices for which
W_{pqrs}\neq 0
, but
|W|^{2}=0
.
This means that the inversion
formula (
7
) for
\overline{W}{}^{pqrs}
, combined with
identity (
35
), cannot work. It remains to check whether there
exist any Weyl tensors that have
|W|^{2}=0
and are generic in our sense
(Section
3.2
).

Note that in the contraction (
48
), only those terms
survive that have total zero boost weight (since scalars can only have
vanishing boost weight). By that argument (or even more directly from formula (
48
)), we can read off that tensors
of Type III (which have only positive boost weight components) will be non-trivial but have
|W|^{2}=0
. The same argument implies that any scalar polynomial invariant built from a Type III Weyl tensor will also vanish, including the cubic invariant
\overset{3}{V}{}_{r}^{r}
, meaning that the canonical inversion from Remark
5.3
(3) also fails.

To verify genericity, We will next show
that the map
\mathrm{W}\colon\mathcal{E}^{s}\to\mathcal{H}_{pqr}[2]\subset\mathcal{E}_{pqr}[2]
for
Type III tensors is injective when
\Psi_{3}\neq 0
, where
\mathcal{H}_{pqr}[2]

denotes the sub-bundle consisting of conformally weighted tensors
satisfying
H_{(pq)r}=H_{[pqr]}=0
and
g^{pr}H_{pqr}=0
. This
will be obvious, once we find the matrix form of
\mathrm{W}
in a basis
adapted to our double null frame.

It is convenient to introduce the special wedge product

(49)

(A\wedge B)_{pqr}=A_{pr}B_{q}-A_{qr}B_{q}.

If
A\in\mathcal{E}_{(pr)}
, then
(A\wedge B)_{(pq)r}=(A\wedge B)_{[pqr]}=0
. A convenient basis for
\mathcal{H}_{pqr}[2]
is the following, where we
group its elements by the indicated boost weight:

(50)

\begin{array}[]{rccccc}-2\colon&(nn)\wedge\bar{m},&(nn)\wedge m,\\
-1\colon&(\bar{m}\bar{m})\wedge n,&2(nm)\wedge\bar{m}+(nn)\wedge l,\\
&2(n\bar{m})\wedge m+(nn)\wedge l,&(mm)\wedge n,\\
0\colon&2(l\bar{m})\wedge n+(\bar{m}\bar{m})\wedge m,&2(n\bar{m})\wedge l+(\bar{m}\bar{m})\wedge m,\\
&2(lm)\wedge n+(mm)\wedge\bar{m},&2(nm)\wedge l+(mm)\wedge\bar{m}\\
1\colon&(\bar{m}\bar{m})\wedge l&2(lm)\wedge\bar{m}+(ll)\wedge n,\\
&2(l\bar{m})\wedge m+(ll)\wedge n,&(mm)\wedge l,\\
2\colon&(ll)\wedge\bar{m}&(ll)\wedge m,\end{array}

where we will order the basis by boost weight with respect to the above
table, in row major order and from left to right within rows.

Since the map
\mathrm{W}
is defined by a contraction, it preserves
boost weights. Moreover, if only the
\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{4}
scalars
parametrizing
W_{pqrs}
are non-vanishing, contracting with the Weyl
tensor can only increase the boost weight by
1
or
2
respectively.
Clearly, the matrix form of
\mathrm{W}
will be block triangular with
respect to subspaces of pure boost weight and checking injectivity will
come down to checking the injectivity of the blocks on the leading
diagonal. Parametrizing vectors as
v^{s}=v_{l}l^{s}+v_{m}m^{s}+v_{m}^{*}\bar{m}^{s}+v_{n}n^{s}
and expressing the contraction
W_{pqrs}v^{s}
in the
basis (
50
) shows that

(51)

\mathrm{W}\begin{bNiceArray}=\begin{bNiceArray}\begin{bNiceArray}.

Indeed we see that the matrix in (
51
) is injective
exactly when
\Psi_{3}\neq 0
. This means that a typical Type III Weyl
tensor is generic in our sense, while having
|W|^{2}=0
. We can also
immediately see that Type N Weyl tensors are not generic.
